Updated 2 memories
Updated 2 memories
React only handles what's on screen and can't safely talk to a database on its own. You need something in between, so the shape is React app → backend/API → database.

Two ways to get the backend

Backend-as-a-service (Firebase or Supabase): They provide the database, user login, and API, and you call them from React with a small library. This is beginner-friendly, and you can have something working in days.
Your own backend (Node.js + Express + PostgreSQL or MongoDB): You write the API routes, queries, and login yourself. This is moderate to hard and takes weeks, but you learn the most.
For a first project I'd start with option 1. Supabase gives you a "real" SQL database, and Firebase is the simplest setup. You can build your own backend later, once the concepts click.

What you need to know

React basics: components, props, useState, useEffect, forms
Async JavaScript: fetch, promises, async/await
CRUD (create, read, update, delete) and basic database ideas (tables/rows or documents)
Authentication, if users have accounts (use the provider's, don't build your own)
A place to deploy, such as Vercel or Netlify
The path

Build a small React app that keeps data in state.
Save it to localStorage so it survives a refresh (a good stepping stone).
Swap in the database: write on form submit, read on page load with useEffect.
Add login so each user sees only their own data.
Deploy.
Two safety rules: never put secret or admin keys in React code, since anyone can see it in the browser, and never store plain-text passwords.

What kind of data do you want to save? That will tell me whether SQL or a Firebase-style database fits better.

Updated memory
Updated memory
Here's how I understand it: admins log in to a web panel, the panel holds student records, and each admin can customize how they view and use that data. I'm assuming students don't log in themselves. Here is everything you'll need, from the ground up.

1. How the pieces fit together
Think of a restaurant. React is the dining room, where people see things and click. The database is the pantry, where everything is stored. The backend is the kitchen staff, who decide who gets what.

Admin's browser (React) → Supabase (checks who you are and what you may do) → database

2. Recommended tools
You won't use all of these at once. Each one shows up in a later phase.

React + Vite: your app. Vite is the modern way to start a React project.
React Router: gives your app "pages" like /login and /students/12.
Mantine or MUI: ready-made buttons, tables, and forms, so you don't design everything from scratch.
TanStack Table: sorting, filtering, and showing/hiding columns, which is the core of personalization.
TanStack Query: fetches data and handles loading, errors, and caching.
React Hook Form + Zod: forms and validation.
Recharts: charts.
PapaParse: reads CSV files, for importing students.
Supabase: the database (PostgreSQL), login system, and security rules in one place. It has a free tier for learning.
Vercel or Netlify: puts your finished app online.
Also needed: Node.js, VS Code, and Git + GitHub.
3. Your data
A database is made of tables, like spreadsheets. Each table is one kind of thing, each row is one item, and each column is one property. Rows connect to each other through IDs.

Suggested tables:

students: id, student code, first/last name, grade, class, guardian contact, created date
scores: student id, subject, term, value (one row per score)
attendance: student id, date, status
profiles: one row per admin, with name and role
user_preferences: admin id plus a block of settings (chosen columns, theme, page size)
saved_views: admin id, view name, and its filters/sort/columns
Scores and attendance get their own tables because one student has many of each. Keeping them separate avoids duplicated data and makes charts easy.

Only collect what you truly need. Every extra field about a student is extra responsibility.

4. Login, roles, and security
This part matters most because it's student data.

Login: use Supabase Auth and turn off public sign-up, so admins are invited rather than self-registered.
Roles: for example admin (everything), staff (view/edit), and viewer (read-only), stored in profiles.
Row Level Security (RLS): rules inside the database, like "only signed-in admins may read students." This is the real lock. Hiding a page in React is only cosmetic, because anyone can bypass the frontend, but not the database rules.
Keys: the public key can live in your React code (RLS protects the data). The secret/service key must never appear there. Anything in a VITE_ environment variable is visible to anyone using browser tools.
Validation: check inputs in the forms and with database constraints (required fields, allowed values).
Safety nets: backups, and a deleted_at column instead of truly deleting rows at first, so mistakes are reversible. An audit log of who changed what can come later.
Privacy law: student data is often legally protected, especially for minors (for example FERPA in the US, GDPR in Europe/UK). I'm not a lawyer, so check with the school or organization about storage, consent, and retention rules.
5. The screens you'll build
Login
Layout with sidebar and top bar
Students table with search, filter, sort, and pagination (load 25–50 rows at a time, not everyone)
Student detail page (info, scores, attendance)
Add/edit form with validation
Delete with a confirmation step
CSV import and export
Dashboard with totals and charts (average score per class, attendance trends)
Settings for personalization, plus user management for admins only
6. Personalization, level by level
"Personalized" can mean a lot of things, so here's a ladder from easy to hard:

Display settings (easy): choose visible columns, sorting, page size, dark mode. Saved in user_preferences.
Saved views (moderate): for example "Grade 10, attendance below 80%", saved under a name.
Custom dashboard (moderate to hard): pick and arrange chart widgets.
Custom fields (hard): admins add their own columns, like "Bus route." This needs a flexible structure, such as a JSON column on students. Build it last, if at all.
Save preferences in the database, not the browser's localStorage, so they follow the admin across devices.

7. Roadmap (rough estimates)
Assuming you study several hours a week:

Phase 0 (1 week): install the tools and create a Vite project.
Phase 1 (2–3 weeks): React fundamentals: components, props, useState, useEffect, lists, and forms. Build small apps first, like a to-do list.
Phase 2 (1–2 weeks): the skeleton with fake data: layout, routing, and a students table from a hardcoded array.
Phase 3 (1–2 weeks): connect Supabase, create the tables, and read students, with loading and error states.
Phase 4 (2 weeks): create, edit, and delete with validated forms.
Phase 5 (1–2 weeks): login, protected routes, roles, and RLS policies. Test by trying to break your own security.
Phase 6 (2–3 weeks): personalization levels 1 and 2.
Phase 7 (2 weeks): dashboard, charts, and CSV import/export.
Phase 8 (1–2 weeks): polish (phone layout, accessibility, clear error messages), basic testing, then deploy.
That's roughly 3–5 months for a solid version. Estimates vary a lot from person to person.

8. Mistakes to avoid
Building everything at once. Finish one slice (like reading students) end to end before starting the next.
Loading every student at once instead of paginating.
Trusting the frontend for security.
Skipping loading and error states.
Using real student data during development. Use fake data until security is finished.
One question that changes the plan: should admins be able to create their own custom fields (like a "Bus route" column), or only choose how the existing data is shown? When you're ready, I can also walk through any phase step by step.

Updated memory
Updated memory
You can build every layer yourself, and it's one of the best ways to learn how real web apps work. Compared with the earlier plan, you'd write the parts Supabase would have handled for you: the server, the database setup, login, and the security rules.

The full picture

Browser (React) → your API server (Node.js + Express) → your database (PostgreSQL)

React shows the screens and sends requests.
The API server checks who's asking and whether they're allowed, then talks to the database. This is the "kitchen staff" from the restaurant picture, except now you hire and train them yourself.
The database stores everything permanently.
What you'd use

Frontend: the same as before (React, Vite, React Router, a component library).
Backend: Node.js + Express. It's JavaScript like React, so it's the smoothest step.
Database: PostgreSQL. Students, scores, and attendance are naturally table-shaped. You'd learn SQL, the language for talking to it.
Login: you build it yourself, covering password hashing, sessions or tokens, and roles.
Tools: Node.js, VS Code, Git/GitHub, PostgreSQL with a viewer like DBeaver or pgAdmin, and an API testing tool like Postman or Bruno.
New skills on top of React

Node/Express: routes, middleware, HTTP status codes
REST API design (for example GET /students, POST /students)
SQL and database design: tables, relationships, constraints, indexes, migrations
Authentication (who you are) vs authorization (what you may do)
Security basics: SQL injection, password storage, CORS, rate limiting, secrets in .env files
Deployment, backups, and logging
Roadmap

Setup and React fundamentals
React skeleton with fake data
Backend basics: a small Express API serving fake students, with React fetching from it
Database: install Postgres, learn SQL, design your tables, and replace the fake data with real queries
Full create/read/update/delete through the API
Login and roles
Security hardening
Personalization, dashboard stats, CSV import/export
Testing, deployment, and backups
Difficulty and time

It's a big jump: roughly 5–8 months at several hours a week, versus 3–5 for the Supabase route. The hardest parts are login/security and deployment. Mistakes there cost the most when the data is about students.

Three suggestions for doing it safely

Design and manage your own database and tables, but host it on a managed Postgres service (such as Neon, Render, or Railway) at first. They handle backups and updates. Running your own database server online is a later, advanced step.
Build the login yourself for learning, but use proven libraries for the sensitive parts (bcrypt or argon2 for passwords, helmet for security headers). Never invent your own encryption.
Use fake data until the security work is done, and have someone experienced review it before real student data goes in.
When you reach any phase, come back and we'll build it together, step by step.

When you're ready, the first session is about 1–2 hours: install Node.js, VS Code, and Git, then get a blank React app running on your screen.

One computer only: no extra time.

Updated memory
Updated memory
At 5 hours a day, the full local version is about 20 working days, which is 4 weeks at 5 days a week. Some days will finish early and others will spill over, so plan for 4–5 weeks and use weekends as buffer.

A typical 5-hour day

30 min: run what we built yesterday and fix anything broken
1 hour: I explain the day's concept and you try a tiny example
3 hours: build the day's feature step by step
30 min: save to Git and note what's next
Week 1: Prototype with fake data
Day 1, setup: Install Node.js, VS Code, Git, and PostgreSQL (plus DBeaver to view the database). Create the Vite React project and GitHub repo. Learn components, JSX, and props.
Day 2, React essentials: useState, lists with .map, events, and form inputs. Build a mini student list in memory.
Day 3, layout and pages: Set up React Router and Mantine. Build the sidebar, top bar, and empty pages (login, students, dashboard, settings).
Day 4, students table: Use TanStack Table with fake data for sorting, search, and show/hide columns. Learn useEffect.
Day 5, forms: Student detail page and an add/edit form (React Hook Form + Zod). Milestone: a clickable prototype.
Week 2: Backend and database
Day 6, first server: Node + Express basics: routes, JSON, status codes. Serve fake students from GET /api/students and fetch them in React with TanStack Query, including loading and error states.
Day 7, SQL basics: SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, WHERE, ORDER BY. Create your database and students table in DBeaver and add rows by hand.
Day 8, connect them: Connect Express to Postgres with the pg library and a .env file. Replace the fake data with real queries, using parameterized queries (this prevents SQL injection). Start numbered migration files for table changes.
Day 9, full CRUD: Add create, update, and delete endpoints (soft delete, so mistakes are reversible), with Zod validation on the server. Wire the React forms to them.
Day 10, related tables: Add scores and attendance with foreign keys and JOINs. The student detail page shows and edits them. Milestone: a real working app, no login yet.
Week 3: Login, roles, and scale
Day 11, login backend: users table, password hashing (argon2 or bcrypt), login/logout endpoints, and sessions in secure cookies. A script creates the first admin.
Day 12, login frontend: Login page, auth context, protected routes, and roles (admin, staff, viewer) enforced on every endpoint. Hiding buttons in React is only cosmetic, so the server does the real checking. We'll test by trying to break it. Milestone: secure login.
Day 13, big tables: Search, filter, sort, and pagination done in SQL (WHERE, LIMIT, indexes), wired to the table.
Day 14, admin management: A page to create admins, assign roles, and deactivate accounts, plus a simple audit log of who changed what.
Day 15, personalization (part 1): A user_preferences table storing each admin's chosen columns, page size, and theme, saved through the API and restored at login.
Week 4: Personalization, dashboard, wrap-up
Day 16, saved views: Save, load, rename, and delete named views (filters, sort, and columns). Milestone: personalization works.
Day 17, dashboard: SQL summaries (average score per class, attendance rates) shown as Recharts charts. Admins choose which widgets appear.
Day 18, CSV import/export: Read files in the browser with PapaParse, show a preview, validate rows, insert in one database transaction, and report errors per row. Add export.
Day 19, hardening and testing: Add helmet and login rate limiting. Review every endpoint's permissions, write a few automated API tests, and polish empty states and phone layout.
Day 20, running it for real: Build the React app and serve it from Express as one program. Set up auto-start, network access if several admins need it, and scheduled backups with pg_dump, including a test restore. Milestone: finished.

Five hours a day of new material is intense. The first days move fast, so some concepts will feel shaky until you use them again. That's normal, and we'll revisit them as they come up.
Custom fields (admins adding their own columns) aren't in this plan and would add a few days.

Read and updated memory
Read and updated memory
Here's the plan: I'll lay out the whole build in 3 steps, then walk through Step 1 in detail. We'll go one step at a time, and you can change anything before we move on.

The plan
Students and their random ID: adding a student by full name.
Parameters and weekly evaluations: defining speed, rhythm, etc., and entering scores each period.
Viewing results: by period (all parameters) or by parameter (all periods).
The one decision that drives everything: each score is stored as its own row: student + parameter + period + value. With that shape, both views in Step 3 are just two different filters on the same table. And a new parameter (say "balance") is just a new row, with no code change. I'll go deeper on this in Step 2.

Project structure
project/
  server/            (Node + Express)
    src/
      routes/        students, parameters, evaluations
      db/            connection, migrations/
      lib/           studentCode (ID generator)
      validation/    rules for incoming data
  client/            (React)
    src/
      api/           functions that call the server
      pages/         Students, StudentDetail, Evaluate, Reports
      components/    tables, forms, charts
Step 1: Students and their random ID
Database table

students
  id            uuid, primary key (Postgres generates it)
  student_code  text, UNIQUE, not null   -- e.g. K7M2QX9A
  full_name     text, not null
  created_at    timestamp, default now()
  deleted_at    timestamp, null          -- soft delete
There are two IDs on purpose. id is internal, and the database uses it to link tables. student_code is the short random code people actually see, read out, and print. A long UUID is unpleasant for humans, and if a code ever had to be reissued, nothing else would break.

Generating the code

8 characters from an alphabet without look-alikes (no 0/O or 1/I): 23456789ABCDEFGHJKLMNPQRSTUVWXYZ. That's about a trillion combinations.
Generated on the server only, using Node's crypto module (not Math.random, which is predictable).
The database's UNIQUE rule is the real guarantee. The code just retries on the rare clash:
createStudent(fullName):
    repeat up to 5 times:
        code = pick 8 random characters
        try INSERT (student_code, full_name)
        if "unique violation": try again
        else: return the new row
    fail with an error
Rules for the design

The code never changes. The API doesn't accept edits to it.
Names are not unique, because two students can share one. That's exactly why the code exists. The form can warn "a student with this name exists, add anyway?"
Names are trimmed, extra spaces collapsed, and 2–100 characters long. Accents and non-Latin letters are allowed.
Deleting is a soft delete, so a code is never reused and old evaluations stay valid.
API routes

POST /api/students takes { fullName } and returns the new student with its code
GET /api/students?search=&page= lists and searches by name or code
GET /api/students/:id fetches one student
PATCH /api/students/:id renames (the code is untouched)
DELETE /api/students/:id soft-deletes
Login isn't part of this test. Later it becomes one checkpoint in front of all these routes, with no rewrites.

React side

StudentsPage holds StudentsTable and an "Add student" button that opens StudentForm.
The table fetches with useQuery. The form submits with useMutation. On success the list refreshes automatically and a message shows the new code with a copy button.
The code is shown grouped (K7M2-QX9A) but stored without the hyphen.
Validation lives in the form for quick feedback and on the server, where it actually counts.
Testing the idea on paper

Add "Maria Lopez": a row appears with a code.
Add another "Maria Lopez": allowed, with a different code.
Whitespace-only name: rejected with a clear message.
Two admins add at the same moment: the database guarantees different codes.
A forced collision in a test: the retry produces a new code.
Rename a student: the code stays the same.
Delete a student: it disappears from the list, but its code stays reserved.
For Step 2, I'll assume the parameters are the same for every student and each score is a number (like 1–10). Tell me if either is wrong. Otherwise, say "next" and we'll go to Step 2.

next

Step 2 is the heart of the app. I'm keeping the earlier assumptions: the same parameters for every student, and numeric values. Here's how the tables connect:

Interactive visual hidden when shared

The middle table, evaluations, is the heart of the app. Each row is one score for one student, on one parameter, in one period. Here is some sample data (the real table stores IDs, but I've used names so it's readable):

student	parameter	period	value
K7M2QX9A	Speed	Week 38	7
K7M2QX9A	Rhythm	Week 38	5
K7M2QX9A	Speed	Week 39	8
P4T9WB2E	Speed	Week 38	6
The diagram shows the core columns. These are the extras:

parameters   + unit (optional), min_value, max_value,
               sort_order
periods      + label ("Week of 14 Sep 2026"),
               UNIQUE (start_date, end_date)
evaluations  + created_at, updated_at,
               UNIQUE (student_id, parameter_id, period_id)
The optional unit and range let a parameter be either a score (0–10) or a measurement (km/h).

Rules for the design

There is one value per student, per parameter, per period, and the UNIQUE rule enforces it. Saving again updates the existing row (an "upsert": insert, or update if it already exists).
Blank means "not measured," never 0. A missing row is a gap on a chart, while a fake 0 would drag averages down.
Parameters are retired (is_active = false), not deleted, so old data keeps its meaning. Once a parameter has data, its range is locked, because changing the scale would make old numbers inconsistent.
Periods are rows, not a hardcoded "weekly." Moving to every two weeks or monthly later needs no redesign. Weeks start on Monday.
The server checks each value against its parameter's range, because the database can't easily check across tables.
API routes

GET /api/parameters, POST /api/parameters, and PATCH /api/parameters/:id (rename, reorder, retire)
GET /api/periods lists periods, newest first
POST /api/periods/ensure takes { date } and finds or creates the period containing that date
GET /api/evaluations?periodId= returns every saved value for a period
PUT /api/evaluations/bulk takes { periodId, entries: [{ studentId, parameterId, value }] }, where a null value clears that cell
Saving logic

saveEvaluations(periodId, entries):
    check the period exists
    load the active parameters (with ranges)
    for each entry:
        student must exist and not be deleted
        parameter must be active
        value must be a number in range (or null)
    if anything is invalid:
        reject everything, list the bad cells
    in ONE transaction:
        value given -> upsert the row
        value null  -> delete the row
    return how many were saved
The transaction makes it all-or-nothing, so a half-saved week can't happen.

React side

ParametersPage (settings) lists, adds, edits, retires, and reorders parameters.
EvaluatePage has a PeriodPicker (defaults to this week, with previous/next arrows) above an EvaluationGrid.
EvaluationGrid has students as rows, active parameters as columns, and number inputs as cells. Edits go into a local draft keyed by student + parameter. Changed cells are highlighted, and Save sends only those in one request.
Out-of-range cells turn red before sending, and the server checks again.
It warns you if you switch period or leave the page with unsaved edits.
Later, the student detail page gets a single-student version of the same form, using the same save route.
Testing the idea on paper

Create Speed (0–10) and Rhythm (0–10): they appear as grid columns.
Open Evaluate: this week's period is created automatically. Enter values for 3 students, save, reload, and the values are still there.
Save a different value for the same cell: it updates, with no duplicate row.
Enter 11 on a 0–10 scale: rejected, and nothing from that save is stored.
Leave a student's cells blank: no rows are created.
Add "Balance" in week 5: earlier weeks just show blank for it, and nothing breaks.
Retire a parameter: it leaves the entry grid, but its history stays available for reports.
Pick last week and enter values: this works as backfill.
Two admins edit the same cell at once: the last save wins. That's fine at this scale, and we can add a "someone changed this" check later.
A deleted student disappears from the grid, but their old evaluations remain.
The size is small for Postgres: 500 students × 10 parameters × 52 weeks is about 260,000 rows a year.

Next is Step 3: viewing by period and by parameter. Say "next," or tell me what to change first.

Explain 2 more beginner-friendly before I think of changes.

Think of Step 2 as a teacher's notebook. Every week you write down how each student did on a few things, like speed and rhythm.

The four lists

A database is a set of lists (called tables), like separate sheets in a spreadsheet. We need four:

Students: who (from Step 1).
Parameters: what we measure, like Speed and Rhythm. The admin can add more later, like Balance.
Periods: when, like Week 38 and Week 39.
Evaluations: the actual scores.
The Evaluations list is the important one. Each row is one small fact:

student	parameter	period	value
K7M2QX9A	Speed	Week 38	7
K7M2QX9A	Rhythm	Week 38	5
K7M2QX9A	Speed	Week 39	8
Read the first row as a sentence: "Student K7M2QX9A scored 7 in Speed during Week 38."

Why one row per score?

The alternative is one wide row per student per week, with a column for each parameter. It looks natural, but adding "Balance" later means rebuilding the table, and "show me one student's Speed over time" gets awkward. With one row per score, nothing needs rebuilding. The two views you asked for (by period, by parameter) become two ways of picking rows from the same list. That's Step 3.

Why separate lists for students, parameters, and periods?

So each thing is written only once. Score rows don't repeat "Speed" or a student's name thousands of times. They point to them by ID, like writing "see student K7M2QX9A". Rename "Speed" in one place and every score follows.

What the admin does

Once: open the Settings page and add parameters, each with a name and an allowed range (like 0 to 10).
Each week: open the Evaluate page. This week is created automatically, and you see a grid with students down the side and parameters across the top.
Type the numbers into the cells and press Save.
Behind the scenes, the server checks every number and saves them all together. If one is wrong (like 11 on a 0–10 scale), nothing is saved, and it tells you which cell to fix.
Simple rules

There is one score per student, per parameter, per week. Saving again replaces the old one.
An empty cell means "not measured," not zero.
Old parameters are hidden, not deleted, so history stays.
"Weekly" isn't fixed. A period is just a row with a start and end date, so switching to every two weeks later is easy.
Things you might want to change

Should every student have the same parameters?
Are all values numbers, or do some need words (like "good" or "needs work")?
Do you want an optional comment with each score?
Is one score per week enough, or might you record several in the same week?
Tell me your changes, or say "next" for Step 3.

matters:
  Period does not need start and end date, only the logic that in this week starting from Saturday  for each parameter there is a place; it means that from Saturday to Friday it is for that period, but it can be implemented later, but once implemented: if duringits week, lock after one week, if outside its own week, lock after one day; so maybe period number x instead of week number x?

Updated 2 memories
Updated 2 memories
Your changes to Step 2
"Period 12" is a better name than "Week 12." It still fits if the length ever changes. Here's the simpler model:

No periods table. One setting stores the Saturday that starts Period 1 (the "anchor"). Every period is calculated from it, Saturday to Friday. For example, with the anchor on Sat 19 Sep 2026, Period 1 runs Sat 19 – Fri 25 Sep, and Period 2 starts Sat 26 Sep.
The current period comes from today's date. The computer's clock decides when it changes, so keep the clock correct.
evaluations stores period_number (1, 2, 3…) instead of a period link. The one-score rule becomes UNIQUE (student_id, parameter_id, period_number), so each student still has one place per parameter per period.
Entries are allowed for Period 1 up to the current one, never future ones.
The anchor is set once and can't change after scores exist, because changing it would shift every period.
The trade-off: calculated dates assume equal, back-to-back periods. Switching to a different length later would need a small conversion of old data.
Locking (later)

Nothing to build now. We already store when each score was first saved and its period number, which is all the rule needs. When we add it:

A score saved during its own period stays editable for 7 days after it was first saved.
A score saved late (outside its own period) stays editable for 1 day.
After that it's read-only. The server enforces this, and the grid just shows a lock icon.
Clearing a cell will blank its value but keep the row, so clearing and retyping can't restart the clock.
Genuine mistakes will need an admin-only override, with a recorded reason.
I read "after one week" as 7 days from the first save, per score. Is that what you meant, or one week after the period ends?

Step 3: Viewing the results
A student's whole history is one grid: periods down, parameters across.

Speed	Rhythm
Period 1	6	4
Period 2	—	5
Period 3	8	6
By period means reading one row (Period 3: Speed 8, Rhythm 6).
By parameter means reading one column (Speed: 6, —, 8).
The database keeps the plain list of scores. React reshapes it into this grid.

One route with optional filters

GET /api/evaluations?studentId=&periodNumber=&parameterId=

Student + period gives the by-period view.
Student + parameter gives the by-parameter view.
Student only gives the whole history.
Period only gives the whole class in one period, which is what the Evaluate grid uses.
GET /api/periods returns the calculated list (number, start date, end date) up to the current period. The anchor is read and set through a settings route.

Queries, in outline

by period:     scores where student = S and period = 12,
               joined to parameters, in sort_order
by parameter:  scores where student = S and parameter = P,
               in period_number order
The UNIQUE rule from Step 2 already gives the database a fast way to find one student's scores. We add one more index (like a book's index) for period-only lookups.

React side

StudentDetailPage shows the name and code, with three tabs: Overview, By period, and By parameter.
It fetches the student's whole history once (about 500 rows a year), so switching tabs is instant.
Two small helper functions reshape the flat list:
byPeriod[12]         = { Speed: 7, Rhythm: 5 }
byParameter["Speed"] = { 1: 6, 3: 8 }
React fills in missing periods itself. They show as "—" in tables and as gaps in charts.
OverviewGrid is the table above.
ByPeriodView has a period picker (latest by default, with dates as a hint), a bar chart, and a table. It can show ▲▼ against the previous period.
ByParameterView has a parameter picker, a line chart across periods (Recharts), a table, and a one-line summary: latest, average, and change since the first score.
Retired parameters stay selectable, marked "(retired)".
After saving in the Evaluate grid, the cached history is refreshed so these views update on their own.
Testing the idea on paper

By period, Period 3: shows Speed 8 and Rhythm 6.
By parameter, Speed: shows one line with a point per period.
A missed period (absent in Period 2): shows "—" and a chart gap, and the average ignores it.
"Balance" added in Period 5: earlier periods show "—" for it, with no errors.
A student with no scores yet: a friendly empty message.
The period picker never offers future periods.
Two students with the same name: kept apart by code.
Scores saved in the Evaluate grid: appear here without a manual refresh.

Updated memory
Updated memory
Quick answers
Why the anchor can't change. Scores only remember a period number, like "Period 3." The anchor date is what turns that number into a real week. With the anchor on Sat 19 Sep, Period 3 is Sat 3 Oct – Fri 9 Oct. If you moved the anchor a week later, Period 3 would silently become 10–16 Oct, and every saved score would now claim to belong to a different week. So it's set once and locked when the first score is saved.

Locking. Confirmed: the clock starts at a score's first save, with 7 days normally and 1 day for a late entry.

Parameters per period
You want four options: add from a period on, remove from a period on, add for one period only, and remove for one period only. The cleanest way is to keep the list of parameters (Speed, Rhythm…) separate and record only the changes in a small log:

parameter_changes
  parameter_id
  period_number
  scope    "from_here_on" or "this_period_only"
  action   "add" or "remove"
  UNIQUE (parameter_id, period_number, scope)
Each of your four options is one row in this log. To find which parameters a period uses, the server applies one rule per parameter:

parametersForPeriod(n):
    for each parameter P:
        find the latest from_here_on change for P
            with period_number <= n
        used = (that change is "add")
        if a this_period_only change exists for P at n:
            used = (that change is "add")   // overrides
        include P if used
Example log:

Speed: add from Period 1 on
Rhythm: add from Period 1 on; remove from Period 10 on
Balance: add from Period 5 on; remove for Period 7 only
Results: Period 3 uses Speed and Rhythm. Period 5 adds Balance. Period 7 drops Balance for that period only. Period 8 has it back. From Period 10, Rhythm is gone.

Why this design

Nothing is copied or prepared per period, so skipped weeks cause no trouble.
You can plan ahead by making a change that starts in a future period.
Undo means deleting one log row.
The history is visible for free.
Rules

A new parameter is created together with its first "add" change, where you choose the starting period.
Scores are never deleted by a parameter change. Removing a parameter from a period that already has scores hides it from the entry grid but keeps the data, and you get a warning first.
The server rejects any score for a parameter that isn't in that period's list.
A "from here on" change lasts until the next change to that parameter, and the history list shows those so nothing surprises you.
Adding a parameter to a past period is allowed. Those scores count as late entries for locking.
This replaces the old "retire" idea: retiring means "remove from Period X on."
In the app

The Evaluate page gets a "Parameters for this period" panel of toggles. Toggling one asks "this period only" or "this period and all following," then Confirm.
A history list shows all changes, each with an Undo button.
In the student views, a blank cell can now mean two things. "—" means it was in use but not measured. A grey "n/a" means the parameter wasn't part of that period. The by-parameter chart only shows periods where the parameter was in use.
The full blueprint
Here's the final data model:

Interactive visual hidden when shared

There's no periods table anymore: period_number is just a number, and the dates come from the anchor. A fifth small table, settings, holds the anchor date. Other details:

students: student_code is unique, plus created_at and deleted_at.
parameters: also holds an optional unit and range (locked once scores exist).
evaluations: UNIQUE (student_id, parameter_id, period_number), plus timestamps. The lock timestamp gets added when we build locking.
Routes

Students: POST, GET (list), GET :id, PATCH :id, DELETE :id on /api/students
Parameters: GET, POST, PATCH on /api/parameters
Changes: GET, POST, DELETE on /api/parameter-changes
Periods: GET /api/periods returns each period from 1 to current, with its dates and its parameter list
Anchor: GET and PUT on /api/settings/period-anchor
Scores: GET /api/evaluations?studentId=&periodNumber=&parameterId= and PUT /api/evaluations/bulk
Screens

Students: list, search, and add.
Student detail: Overview, By period, By parameter.
Evaluate: period picker, the "Parameters for this period" panel, and the entry grid.
Parameters: the list, adding one (with its starting period), and the change history with Undo.
Settings: set the anchor (first run only).
Build order

Setup: tools, folders, Postgres, a server that answers, and a React page showing its answer.
Students: the code generator, routes, list, and add form. Done when 3 students get 3 different codes.
Period logic: the anchor setting plus two small functions (date to period number, number to dates). Add a few automated tests, such as Fri 25 Sep → Period 1 and Sat 26 Sep → Period 2.
Parameters and per-period changes: the list, the change log, and parametersForPeriod, tested with the example above.
Evaluate: saving and loading scores, the grid, and the period parameters panel.
Student views: the history route, Overview, By period, and By parameter with charts.
Polish: empty states, clear errors, "n/a" vs "—", and the warnings.
Steps 3 and 4 are worth testing first. They're small pure calculations where dates and rules cause the sneakiest bugs.

Left for later, outside this test: locking, login and roles, personalization, dashboard, CSV, and backups.

Rough size: about 60–80 hours, or about 3 weeks at 5 hours a day.

Piece 3: the database
We'll create a database for the project and a separate user for the app. Using the all-powerful postgres admin account would be riskier, since a mistake or leak could touch everything. A limited user can only touch this one database.

A. Create the user and database

Open the database's command prompt:

sudo -u postgres psql
The prompt changes to postgres=#. Type these lines there:

CREATE USER student_app;
CREATE DATABASE student_panel OWNER student_app;
\password student_app
\q
SQL lines end with ;. The backslash commands (\password, \q) don't.
The first two lines should answer CREATE ROLE and CREATE DATABASE.
\password asks you to type the password twice, with nothing shown on screen. Use letters and numbers only, 16 or more characters, because symbols cause trouble in connection addresses later. An easy way is to run openssl rand -hex 16 in the terminal first and use its output.
Keep the password somewhere safe. It goes into a .env file in piece 4, and never into Git.
OWNER lets student_app create tables in its own database.
B. Check the app user can log in

psql -h localhost -U student_app -d student_panel -c "SELECT current_user, current_database(), gen_random_uuid();"
It asks for the password. You should see one row: student_app, student_panel, and a long random ID. That single check proves three things: the login works, the database exists, and the random-ID function we need for students is there.

-h localhost matches how our server will connect. Without it you'll get "Peer authentication failed," which is expected and harmless.
"Password authentication failed" means the password doesn't match, so redo the \password line.

Piece 4: the server
A. Create the server folder and install the libraries

npm init -y creates package.json, the project's info file.
express is the web server library, and pg lets Node talk to PostgreSQL.
type=module lets us use modern import syntax, the same as React.
The dev script runs the server. --watch restarts it whenever you save a file, and --env-file=.env loads your secrets from a file.
B. Store the database password

Create the file server/.env (in your editor, or run nano .env, type, then Ctrl+O, Enter, Ctrl+X to save and exit) with exactly these two lines, using your real password:

DATABASE_URL=postgresql://student_app:YOUR_PASSWORD@localhost:5432/student_panel
PORT=3000
The address reads as postgresql://USER:PASSWORD@HOST:PORT/DATABASE. Now check that Git will ignore this file:

git check-ignore .env
It should print .env. If it prints nothing, stop and tell me.

C. Write two small files

mkdir -p src/db
server/src/db/pool.js:

js
import pg from 'pg';

const pool = new pg.Pool({
  connectionString: process.env.DATABASE_URL,
});

export default pool;
A "pool" is a small set of ready-to-use database connections. Reusing them is much faster than opening a new one for every request.

server/src/index.js:

js
import express from 'express';
import pool from './db/pool.js';

const app = express();
app.use(express.json());

app.get('/api/health', (req, res) => {
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

app.get('/api/db-check', async (req, res) => {
  const result = await pool.query('SELECT now() AS server_time');
  res.json(result.rows[0]);
});

const port = process.env.PORT || 3000;
app.listen(port, () => {
  console.log(`Server running at http://localhost:${port}`);
});
Each app.get(...) is a route: an address plus what to do when someone visits it.

/api/health just answers "I'm alive."
/api/db-check asks the database for the time, which proves server → database works.
D. Run and test

npm run dev
You should see Server running at http://localhost:3000. Leave it running. Open a second terminal tab (Ctrl+Shift+T) and run:

curl http://localhost:3000/api/health
curl http://localhost:3000/api/db-check
Expected answers: {"ok":true}, then {"server_time":"2026-..."}. Stop the server with Ctrl+C in the first tab.

If something goes wrong

"password authentication failed": the password in .env doesn't match the one you set in piece 3.
"ECONNREFUSED": PostgreSQL isn't running. Try sudo systemctl start postgresql.
"bad option: --env-file": your Node is too old. Send me node --version.
"Cannot use import statement outside a module": the type=module line didn't apply. Send me package.json.

Piece 5: the React page

B. Let React reach the server

Open client/vite.config.js. Leave the top lines as they are, and add the server part after the plugins line:

js
export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [react()],
  server: {
    proxy: {
      '/api': 'http://localhost:3000',
    },
  },
})
Your browser will load the React app from Vite (port 5173), and your server runs on port 3000. This "proxy" tells Vite to pass anything starting with /api on to the server. The browser only ever talks to one address, which will make login much simpler later.

C. Replace the page

Replace everything in client/src/App.jsx with:

jsx
import { useEffect, useState } from 'react';

async function getJson(url) {
  const res = await fetch(url);
  if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`${url} failed with status ${res.status}`);
  return res.json();
}

function App() {
  const [health, setHealth] = useState(null);
  const [dbTime, setDbTime] = useState(null);
  const [error, setError] = useState(null);

  useEffect(() => {
    async function load() {
      try {
        setHealth(await getJson('/api/health'));
        setDbTime(await getJson('/api/db-check'));
      } catch (err) {
        setError(`${err.message}. Is the server running?`);
      }
    }
    load();
  }, []);

  if (error) return <p>{error}</p>;
  if (!health || !dbTime) return <p>Loading...</p>;

  return (
    <div>
      <h1>Student panel</h1>
      <p>Server says: {JSON.stringify(health)}</p>
      <p>Database time: {dbTime.server_time}</p>
    </div>
  );
}

export default App;
How it works:

useState is the component's memory. Each of those three values starts empty.
useEffect(..., []) runs once, right after the page first appears. That's where we ask the server for data.
When an answer arrives, setHealth and setDbTime fill the memory and React redraws the page.
The two if (...) return lines show "Loading..." or an error until there's something to display.
D. Run everything

You need two terminal tabs open at once.

Tab 1 (the server):

cd ~/projects/student-panel/server
npm run dev
Tab 2 (React), opened with Ctrl+Shift+T:

cd ~/projects/student-panel/client
npm run dev
Tab 2 prints a line like Local: http://localhost:5173/. Open that address in your browser. You should see:

Student panel
Server says: {"ok":true}
Database time: 2026-...
Then try the error path. Stop the server with Ctrl+C in tab 1 and refresh the page. You should see a message like "/api/health failed with status 500. Is the server running?" Start the server again afterwards.

If something goes wrong

An error message on the page while the server is running: check that PORT=3000 in server/.env matches the proxy address.
A blank page: press F12 in the browser, open the Console tab, and send me the red text.
Vite says the port is busy: it picks another one and prints the address to use.
npm complains about the Node version: send me node --version.

cd ~/projects/student-panel
git add .

The list should include the client files but not node_modules or .env. Then:

Here's the whole setup in one picture, then each piece in detail with a correctness check.

Interactive visual hidden when shared

The arrows show requests going down. The answers come back up the same path. There are also two separate projects inside one folder: server/ and client/. Each has its own package.json and its own node_modules, and Git tracks the whole folder.

Piece 1: the tools
Git keeps a history of your code, so you can go back if something breaks.
curl downloads things from the internet using the terminal. We used it to fetch nvm's installer.
Node.js lets JavaScript run outside a browser. Your server runs on it, and so do React's build tools.
nvm installs Node into your home folder, so it needs no admin rights and lets you switch versions later. --lts picks the stable long-term-support version.
npm comes with Node. It downloads libraries and records them in package.json.
PostgreSQL is the database program. It runs quietly in the background as a service and starts with your computer. systemctl is-active asks whether that service is running. sudo -u postgres psql -c "..." runs one command as the database's admin account.
VS Code is just the editor.
Check: correct. The version in the nvm address is only the installer's own version and still installs the latest LTS Node. PostgreSQL's version depends on your Mint release (14 or 16), and both work, since the random-ID function we need exists from version 13.

Piece 2: folder and Git
git config --global ... sets the name and email attached to your saves. init.defaultBranch main names the main line of history main.
git init creates a hidden .git folder. That folder is the history.
.gitignore lists what Git must never save: node_modules/ (huge and rebuildable) and .env (secrets).
Saving takes two steps. git add . puts changes into a box, and git commit seals and labels the box. git log --oneline lists the saves.
Check: correct. If you ever publish the project on GitHub, the name and email in your saves become visible.

Piece 3: the database
The layers are: the PostgreSQL program, then databases inside it, then tables inside each database.

We made a separate user, student_app, that can only touch student_panel. If the app ever leaks its password or has a bug, the damage stays inside that one database instead of reaching everything.
CREATE USER and CREATE DATABASE ... OWNER student_app are SQL, so they end with ;. \password and \q are shortcuts of the psql program itself, so they don't.
OWNER matters because in PostgreSQL 15 and later only a database's owner may create tables in its default area. Because student_app owns the database, the app can create its own tables later without admin rights.
The test login used -h localhost, which is the way our server will connect, and that requires the password. Without it, PostgreSQL tries "peer" login (matching your Linux username) and refuses. That refusal is expected.
gen_random_uuid() is the random-ID generator we'll use for students.
Check: correct. PostgreSQL only accepts connections from your own computer by default, which is what we want, so leave that alone.

Piece 4: the server
npm init -y creates package.json. npm install express pg downloads the two libraries into node_modules and records them in package.json.
type=module allows modern import syntax, the same style React uses.
The dev script runs node --watch --env-file=.env src/index.js. --watch restarts the server when you save a code file. --env-file loads the lines of .env so the code can read them.
.env holds settings that vary by machine or must stay secret. DATABASE_URL reads as postgresql://USER:PASSWORD@HOST:PORT/DATABASE.
pool.js creates one shared pool of ready-made database connections. Reusing them is much faster than opening a new one per request.
In index.js:
express() creates the app.
app.use(express.json()) teaches it to read JSON sent to it, which we'll need for adding students.
app.get('/api/health', handler) is a route: an address plus what to do when it's visited. The handler receives req (the request) and res (the response).
async and await let the code wait for the database without freezing.
pool.query(...) returns an object whose rows are the results, and res.json(...) sends an answer back as JSON.
app.listen(port) starts the server.
Check: correct. Three things to know:

--watch only watches code. If you edit .env, stop the server (Ctrl+C) and run npm run dev again.
app.listen(port) listens on every network interface, so other computers on your network could reach port 3000. That's harmless now with two test routes. Once real student routes exist they need login, and we'll delete /api/db-check then.
Current Express (version 5) turns a failed database call into a 500 error automatically instead of hanging.
Piece 5: the React page
Vite is a dev server plus build tool. It serves your files and refreshes the browser when you save. create vite generates the starter: index.html loads src/main.jsx, which draws App on the page.
The proxy is needed because the page comes from port 5173 and the server is on 3000. Browsers treat different ports as different sites and restrict cross-site requests (called CORS). With the proxy, the page only ever talks to its own address, and Vite forwards /api calls. This will also keep login cookies simple later.
In App.jsx:
A component is a function that returns JSX, which looks like HTML.
useState gives it a memory. When you set a value, React redraws the page.
useEffect(..., []) runs once after the first display, which is where we ask the server for data.
fetch only fails on network problems, not on server errors like 500. That's why getJson checks res.ok itself.
The if (...) return lines show an error or "Loading..." until the data arrives.
Two terminals run because there are two programs on two ports.
Check: correct. In development mode React may run the effect twice, which is harmless. The proxy also exists only while developing. In the final local install, Express will serve the built React files itself, so it becomes one program on one port.

from piece 1 start and explain what each step we make is and what each code or action does. for now piece 1.

Piece 1 puts four kinds of software on your laptop: Git, Node.js, PostgreSQL, and a code editor. After each install we run a small check that prints a version number. That proves the tool is installed and the terminal can find it.

Three terminal basics first
The terminal is a text window where you type an instruction and press Enter. A line looks like program -option value: the first word is the program to run, and the rest tell it how. The prompt (you@laptop:~$) means "ready." The ~ stands for your home folder.
Paste with Ctrl+Shift+V. Ctrl+C stops a running program (it doesn't copy).
sudo means "run this with administrator rights." It asks for your password, and nothing shows as you type. That's normal, so type it and press Enter. Installing system-wide software needs it.
A. Git and curl
sudo apt update
sudo apt install -y git curl
git --version
apt is Linux Mint's app installer. It downloads software from official servers and installs whatever that software depends on.
sudo apt update refreshes apt's list of what's available and which versions. It installs nothing. Skipping it can cause "package not found" errors because the list is out of date. You'll see lines starting with Hit: or Get:, and a note like "N packages can be upgraded," which you can ignore.
sudo apt install -y git curl installs two programs. -y answers "yes" to apt's "Do you want to continue?" question. If they're already installed, it says "already the newest version," which is fine.
Git keeps a history of your code, and we use it in piece 2.
curl downloads things from an address, straight from the terminal. We need it in step B.
git --version asks Git to print its version. Almost every tool answers --version, so it's the standard "is it installed?" check. Expect something like git version 2.x.x.
B. Node.js through nvm
curl -o- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/nvm-sh/nvm/v0.40.1/install.sh | bash
Why Node: JavaScript was built to run inside browsers. Node is that same engine packaged to run on your computer. Your server will run on it, and React's development tools run on it too (React itself still runs in the browser). Why nvm: it keeps Node in your home folder, so it needs no sudo, and it makes switching or updating versions easy.

Reading this line from left to right:

The address points to nvm's official installer script on GitHub. v0.40.1 is the installer's version.
curl downloads it. -o- means "send the download to the screen instead of saving a file." Technically, -o names an output file, and the name - means "standard output."
| (a pipe) hands that output to the next program. bash runs whatever it receives as a script. So the line means "download and run at once."
Running a downloaded script deserves care. This one is the official, widely used installer, and it only touches your home folder. In general, only do this with sources you trust. The safer habit is to download it to a file, read it, then run it.
What the script does: it puts nvm into a hidden folder, ~/.nvm. It also adds a few lines to ~/.bashrc, a file the terminal reads each time it opens, so the nvm command exists. It ends by telling you to close and reopen the terminal, because the current window already read .bashrc before the change. Opening a fresh one loads it. (Optional check in the new window: nvm --version should print 0.40.1.)

nvm install --lts
node --version
npm --version
nvm install --lts fetches the newest LTS ("long-term support") version of Node, downloads it, and makes it your default. Node has "Current" versions with the newest features and shorter support, and LTS versions that get security and bug fixes for about 2.5 years. LTS is the right choice for a project we want to keep stable. It ends with something like Now using node v24.x.x.
node --version prints the Node version, like v24.x.x. The exact number depends on the day you install, and any LTS number is fine.
npm --version prints the version of npm, which came with Node. npm is Node's library manager. It downloads code other people published (Express, React, and so on) and records what your project uses.
C. PostgreSQL
sudo apt install -y postgresql postgresql-contrib
postgresql is the database server, plus its command-line client, psql. postgresql-contrib holds optional extras. In recent versions those are already included, so it may install almost nothing, which is harmless.
The install does more than copy files. It creates a Linux user named postgres, creates the storage area where your data will live, and registers PostgreSQL as a background service that starts with your computer. It listens for connections from your own computer only.
sudo systemctl is-active postgresql
systemctl is the control panel for background services on Mint. is-active asks whether a service is running, and postgresql is the service's name. It should print active. If it prints inactive or failed, run sudo systemctl start postgresql and check again.
sudo -u postgres psql -c "SELECT version();"
sudo -u postgres runs the command as the Linux user postgres. That user owns the database files and is the built-in database admin. PostgreSQL trusts it automatically for connections from the same computer, so there is no database password yet.
psql is PostgreSQL's command-line client, and -c "..." means "run this one command, then exit."
SELECT version(); is SQL, the language databases speak. SELECT means "give me," and version() is a built-in function. The ; ends the statement.
The answer is a small table with one row, like "PostgreSQL 16.x on x86_64...". Seeing it proves the server is running and a client can talk to it.
D. Code editor (optional)
An editor is where you'll write code. VS Code is our suggestion, but any editor works.

sudo apt install ./Downloads/code_*.deb
code --version
A .deb file is a downloadable installer package for Debian-family systems (Mint included). Your browser saves it in ~/Downloads.
The ./ before Downloads tells apt "this is a file on my computer, not a program name to look up online." Without it, apt fails.
code_*.deb uses a wildcard. The * stands for any characters, so it matches a real filename like code_1.105.0-..._amd64.deb. If two VS Code downloads sit in that folder, it gets confused, so type the exact filename (list them with ls ~/Downloads).
The installer usually also sets up automatic updates through Mint's normal updater.
code --version prints a few lines, starting with the version number.
If something goes wrong
"incorrect password": just try again.
"Could not get lock ... dpkg": another installer or the Update Manager is busy. Wait a minute or close it, then retry.
"Unable to locate package": run sudo apt update again and check that you're online.
"Could not resolve host" from curl: no internet connection.
nvm: command not found: the terminal wasn't reopened. Close it and open a new one, or run source ~/.bashrc.
psql "connection ... failed": the database service isn't running. Start it with sudo systemctl start postgresql.
"Unable to locate ... code_*.deb": the file isn't in ~/Downloads, so check with ls ~/Downloads.

With the setup done, we start Step 1 of the design: students with their random codes. I'll split it into pieces so each ends with something you can check:

Piece 6: the students table
Instead of creating tables by typing SQL into psql, we'll write each change as a numbered file, called a migration. A small script applies any new files in order and remembers which ones ran. That way the structure is saved in Git, and you can rebuild the whole database on a new computer by running one command.

A. Create the folder

cd /data/projects/student-panel/server
mkdir -p src/db/migrations
B. The first migration

Create server/src/db/migrations/001_create_students.sql:

sql
CREATE TABLE students (
  id           uuid PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
  student_code text NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  full_name    text NOT NULL,
  created_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  deleted_at   timestamptz,
  CONSTRAINT student_code_format
    CHECK (student_code ~ '^[2-9A-HJ-NP-Z]{8}$'),
  CONSTRAINT full_name_length
    CHECK (char_length(btrim(full_name)) BETWEEN 2 AND 100)
);
What each part does:

id ... PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid() is the internal ID. The database makes a random one for every new row.
student_code ... UNIQUE is the short code people see. UNIQUE means the database refuses a duplicate, which is the real guarantee of uniqueness.
NOT NULL means the field is required.
timestamptz is a date and time that includes the time zone. DEFAULT now() fills it in automatically.
deleted_at stays empty for active students. Deleting later means filling it in (a "soft delete").
The two CHECK rules are a last line of defense. The code must be exactly 8 characters from our alphabet (no 0, 1, I, or O), and the name must be 2 to 100 characters after trimming spaces. Our server will validate first, and the database backs it up.
C. The migration runner

Create server/src/db/migrate.js:

js
import fs from 'node:fs/promises';
import path from 'node:path';
import pool from './pool.js';

const migrationsDir = path.join(import.meta.dirname, 'migrations');

async function migrate() {
  await pool.query(`
    CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS schema_migrations (
      filename   text PRIMARY KEY,
      applied_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
    )
  `);

  const applied = await pool.query('SELECT filename FROM schema_migrations');
  const done = new Set(applied.rows.map((row) => row.filename));

  const files = (await fs.readdir(migrationsDir))
    .filter((name) => name.endsWith('.sql'))
    .sort();

  let appliedCount = 0;
  for (const file of files) {
    if (done.has(file)) continue;

    const sql = await fs.readFile(path.join(migrationsDir, file), 'utf8');
    const client = await pool.connect();
    try {
      await client.query('BEGIN');
      await client.query(sql);
      await client.query('INSERT INTO schema_migrations (filename) VALUES ($1)', [file]);
      await client.query('COMMIT');
      console.log(`Applied ${file}`);
      appliedCount++;
    } catch (err) {
      await client.query('ROLLBACK');
      console.error(`Failed on ${file}: ${err.message}`);
      process.exitCode = 1;
      break;
    } finally {
      client.release();
    }
  }

  if (appliedCount === 0 && !process.exitCode) {
    console.log('Database is up to date.');
  }
  await pool.end();
}

migrate();
What it does, in order:

It creates a small schema_migrations table that lists the files already applied.
It reads that list, then reads the .sql files in the folder, sorted by name (that's why they start with 001, 002, and so on).
For each file not yet applied, it runs the SQL inside a transaction. BEGIN and COMMIT make it all-or-nothing, so a failing file leaves nothing half-done, and ROLLBACK undoes it.
On success it records the filename. On failure it prints the error and stops.
pool.end() closes the connections so the script can exit.
One rule: never edit a migration file after it has run. Any later change goes into a new file (002, 003...).

D. Run it

npm pkg set scripts.migrate="node --env-file=.env src/db/migrate.js"
npm run migrate
npm run migrate
The first run should print Applied 001_create_students.sql. The second should print Database is up to date. That proves it doesn't repeat itself.

The first insert prints INSERT 0 1, and the SELECT shows the row with a random id.
The second insert fails with duplicate key value violates unique constraint.
The third fails with violates check constraint "student_code_format".
The fourth fails with violates check constraint "full_name_length".

Piece 7: the code generator and student routes
You'll add three new files and replace one. From the server folder:

cd /data/projects/student-panel/server
mkdir -p src/lib src/validation src/routes
A. The code generator

Create src/lib/studentCode.js:

js
import { randomInt } from 'node:crypto';

const ALPHABET = '23456789ABCDEFGHJKLMNPQRSTUVWXYZ';
const CODE_LENGTH = 8;

export function generateStudentCode() {
  let code = '';
  for (let i = 0; i < CODE_LENGTH; i++) {
    code += ALPHABET[randomInt(ALPHABET.length)];
  }
  return code;
}
The alphabet has 32 characters: digits 2–9 and letters, without look-alikes (no 0, 1, I, or O). This matches the database rule from piece 6.
randomInt(32) picks a number from 0 to 31 using the computer's secure random source. Math.random() is predictable, so we don't use it for IDs.
The loop picks 8 characters. That gives 32⁸, about 1.1 trillion possible codes.
Test it:

node --input-type=module -e "import { generateStudentCode } from './src/lib/studentCode.js'; console.log(Array.from({ length: 5 }, () => generateStudentCode()).join(' '))"
You should get five different 8-character codes.

B. Name checking

Create src/validation/student.js:

js
export function normalizeFullName(input) {
  if (typeof input !== 'string') {
    return { error: 'Full name is required.' };
  }
  const name = input.trim().replace(/\s+/g, ' ');
  if (name.length < 2) {
    return { error: 'Full name must be at least 2 characters.' };
  }
  if (name.length > 100) {
    return { error: 'Full name must be 100 characters or fewer.' };
  }
  return { value: name };
}
.trim() removes spaces at the ends, and .replace(/\s+/g, ' ') turns any run of spaces into one. So "  Maria   Lopez " becomes "Maria Lopez".
The function returns either { error } or { value }, so the caller can check which one it got.
It doesn't restrict which letters are allowed, so accents and non-Latin names work.
C. The student routes

Create src/routes/students.js:

js
import { Router } from 'express';
import pool from '../db/pool.js';
import { generateStudentCode } from '../lib/studentCode.js';
import { normalizeFullName } from '../validation/student.js';

const router = Router();

const UNIQUE_VIOLATION = '23505';

const STUDENT_COLUMNS = `
  id,
  student_code AS "studentCode",
  full_name    AS "fullName",
  created_at   AS "createdAt"
`;

router.post('/', async (req, res) => {
  const parsed = normalizeFullName(req.body?.fullName);
  if (parsed.error) {
    return res.status(400).json({ error: parsed.error });
  }

  for (let attempt = 1; attempt <= 5; attempt++) {
    try {
      const result = await pool.query(
        `INSERT INTO students (student_code, full_name)
         VALUES ($1, $2)
         RETURNING ${STUDENT_COLUMNS}`,
        [generateStudentCode(), parsed.value]
      );
      return res.status(201).json(result.rows[0]);
    } catch (err) {
      if (err.code !== UNIQUE_VIOLATION) throw err;
    }
  }

  res.status(500).json({ error: 'Could not create a unique student code. Please try again.' });
});

router.get('/', async (req, res) => {
  const result = await pool.query(
    `SELECT ${STUDENT_COLUMNS}
     FROM students
     WHERE deleted_at IS NULL
     ORDER BY created_at DESC
     LIMIT 500`
  );
  res.json({ students: result.rows });
});

export default router;
What it does:

A Router is a mini-app for related routes. We'll attach it at /api/students, so router.post('/') means POST /api/students.
Creating a student:
It checks the name first. A bad name gets a 400 ("your request is wrong") with a clear message.
Then it tries the INSERT up to 5 times. Each try uses a fresh random code.
If the database says the code already exists (error code 23505, "unique violation"), the loop simply tries a new code. This is the retry from the design. Any other error is thrown to the server's general error handler.
RETURNING makes the database send back the new row, so we don't need a second query.
Success answers 201 Created.
Safety rule: the name goes in through $1 and $2, never pasted into the SQL text. This stops "SQL injection," where a hostile input like '; DROP TABLE students; -- would run as a command. ${STUDENT_COLUMNS} is safe only because it's a fixed text we wrote ourselves.
AS "studentCode" renames columns from the database style (student_code) to the JavaScript style (studentCode).
Listing students:
It returns only active students (deleted_at IS NULL), newest first.
The LIMIT 500 is a temporary safety cap.
The answer is wrapped as { students: [...] }, so we can add totals and page numbers later without changing its shape. Real pagination and search arrive in piece 9.
D. Connect it and add an error handler

Replace the whole of src/index.js with:

js
import express from 'express';
import pool from './db/pool.js';
import studentsRouter from './routes/students.js';

const app = express();
app.use(express.json());

app.get('/api/health', (req, res) => {
  res.json({ ok: true });
});

app.get('/api/db-check', async (req, res) => {
  const result = await pool.query('SELECT now() AS server_time');
  res.json(result.rows[0]);
});

app.use('/api/students', studentsRouter);

app.use((err, req, res, next) => {
  const status = err.status || 500;
  if (status >= 500) console.error(err);
  res.status(status).json({
    error: status >= 500 ? 'Something went wrong on the server.' : err.message,
  });
});

const port = process.env.PORT || 3000;
app.listen(port, () => {
  console.log(`Server running at http://localhost:${port}`);
});
The new pieces are the app.use('/api/students', ...) line and the error handler at the bottom.

An error handler is a function with four parameters, and Express recognizes it by that shape. It must come after the routes.
Problems caused by the request itself, like broken JSON, have a status below 500, so their message is shown. For real server faults, the details go into the server's log only, and the client just gets a generic message. Hiding internals is a small security habit.

curl -s -w "\nHTTP %{http_code}\n" -X POST http://localhost:3000/api/students -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{oops'
curl -s http://localhost:3000/api/students
The flags: -s hides the progress bar, -w adds the status code after the answer, -X POST picks the method, -H sets a header (it says "the body is JSON"), and -d is the body.

Piece 8 builds on piece 7, so the server must have the student routes. It adds a page with a form to add students and a table that lists them. It replaces the piece 5 test page.

To keep this piece simple, we use plain React plus one library, TanStack Query, which handles loading, errors, and refreshing the list. Piece 8: the students page
A. Install and create folders

cd /data/projects/student-panel/client
npm install @tanstack/react-query
mkdir -p src/api src/lib src/components src/pages
B. Talking to the server

src/api/http.js:

js
export async function request(url, options = {}) {
  let response;
  try {
    response = await fetch(url, {
      headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
      ...options,
    });
  } catch {
    throw new Error('Cannot reach the server. Is it running?');
  }

  let data = null;
  try {
    data = await response.json();
  } catch {
    // the answer had no JSON body
  }

  if (!response.ok) {
    throw new Error(
      data?.error ??
        `Request failed (status ${response.status}). If this keeps happening, check that the server is running.`
    );
  }
  return data;
}
src/api/students.js:

js
import { request } from './http.js';

export async function listStudents() {
  const data = await request('/api/students');
  return data.students;
}

export function createStudent(fullName) {
  return request('/api/students', {
    method: 'POST',
    body: JSON.stringify({ fullName }),
  });
}
http.js is the one place that talks to the server. fetch only fails on network problems, not on errors like 400 or 500, so this helper checks response.ok and turns the server's { error: "..." } message into a normal JavaScript error.
students.js gives each operation a name. Components call listStudents() and never need to know the address.
src/lib/format.js:

js
export function formatStudentCode(code) {
  return `${code.slice(0, 4)}-${code.slice(4)}`;
}

export function formatDateTime(isoString) {
  return new Date(isoString).toLocaleString();
}
The database stores the code without a hyphen (K7M2QX9A), and we display it grouped (K7M2-QX9A) because it's easier to read and read aloud.

C. Turn on TanStack Query

Replace all of src/main.jsx with:

jsx
import { StrictMode } from 'react';
import { createRoot } from 'react-dom/client';
import { QueryClient, QueryClientProvider } from '@tanstack/react-query';
import './index.css';
import App from './App.jsx';

const queryClient = new QueryClient({
  defaultOptions: { queries: { retry: 1 } },
});

createRoot(document.getElementById('root')).render(
  <StrictMode>
    <QueryClientProvider client={queryClient}>
      <App />
    </QueryClientProvider>
  </StrictMode>
);
The QueryClient is TanStack Query's memory of everything fetched. QueryClientProvider makes it available to every component. retry: 1 means a failed request is tried once more instead of the default three times, so errors show up faster while developing.

D. The form

src/components/StudentForm.jsx:

jsx
import { useState } from 'react';
import { useMutation, useQueryClient } from '@tanstack/react-query';
import { createStudent } from '../api/students.js';
import { formatStudentCode } from '../lib/format.js';

function StudentForm() {
  const queryClient = useQueryClient();
  const [fullName, setFullName] = useState('');
  const [problem, setProblem] = useState(null);

  const mutation = useMutation({
    mutationFn: createStudent,
    onSuccess: () => {
      setFullName('');
      queryClient.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['students'] });
    },
  });

  function handleSubmit(event) {
    event.preventDefault();
    const name = fullName.trim().replace(/\s+/g, ' ');
    if (name.length < 2 || name.length > 100) {
      mutation.reset();
      setProblem('Full name must be between 2 and 100 characters.');
      return;
    }
    setProblem(null);
    mutation.mutate(name);
  }

  return (
    <form onSubmit={handleSubmit}>
      <label htmlFor="fullName">Full name</label>
      <div className="row">
        <input
          id="fullName"
          value={fullName}
          onChange={(event) => setFullName(event.target.value)}
          placeholder="e.g. Maria Lopez"
          maxLength={200}
          autoComplete="off"
        />
        <button type="submit" disabled={mutation.isPending}>
          {mutation.isPending ? 'Adding...' : 'Add student'}
        </button>
      </div>

      {problem && <p className="error">{problem}</p>}
      {mutation.isError && <p className="error">{mutation.error.message}</p>}
      {mutation.isSuccess && (
        <p className="success">
          Added {mutation.data.fullName} with code{' '}
          <strong>{formatStudentCode(mutation.data.studentCode)}</strong>
        </p>
      )}
    </form>
  );
}

export default StudentForm;
The input is "controlled." React's state (fullName) holds the text, and value plus onChange keep the two in sync.
event.preventDefault() stops the browser's default form behavior, which would reload the page.
useMutation is for changes (a POST), where useQuery is for reading. mutation.mutate(name) sends the request, and the isPending, isError, and isSuccess flags drive the button and messages.
On success, invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['students'] }) tells TanStack Query "the list is out of date, fetch it again." The table redraws by itself, so we never update it by hand.
The check in handleSubmit is only a convenience for instant feedback. The server does the real checking.
E. The table

src/components/StudentsTable.jsx:

jsx
import { useQuery } from '@tanstack/react-query';
import { listStudents } from '../api/students.js';
import { formatStudentCode, formatDateTime } from '../lib/format.js';

function StudentsTable() {
  const {
    data: students,
    isPending,
    isError,
    error,
  } = useQuery({
    queryKey: ['students'],
    queryFn: listStudents,
  });

  if (isPending) return <p>Loading students...</p>;
  if (isError) return <p className="error">{error.message}</p>;
  if (students.length === 0) {
    return <p>No students yet. Add the first one above.</p>;
  }

  return (
    <table>
      <thead>
        <tr>
          <th>Code</th>
          <th>Full name</th>
          <th>Added</th>
        </tr>
      </thead>
      <tbody>
        {students.map((student) => (
          <tr key={student.id}>
            <td className="code">{formatStudentCode(student.studentCode)}</td>
            <td>{student.fullName}</td>
            <td>{formatDateTime(student.createdAt)}</td>
          </tr>
        ))}
      </tbody>
    </table>
  );
}

export default StudentsTable;
useQuery fetches the data, keeps it in the cache, and reports its state. queryKey: ['students'] is the label for this data, which is what invalidateQueries in the form refers to.
isPending is true until the first answer arrives. The three if (...) return lines cover loading, error, and empty cases before the real table.
.map turns each student into a table row. key={student.id} lets React track each row across redraws, so it must be unique and stable.
